People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF90.8, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I’ve noticed over the past year or so that I’m just always doing something. Puttering around the house, fixing little things, arranging this or that, saying yes to one more volunteer thing. \n\nI know that may not sound terrible, and I do like being useful, but even at my age I seem to have a hard time just sitting down and being still. I wasn’t sure if that’s worth coming in for, but it’s been on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious or worried when you're not doing something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I don’t sit there worrying, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more just… I feel better if my hands are busy, I suppose. But I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be trying to avoid something by keeping yourself busy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least, I’m not aware of avoiding anything in particular. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired?\n\nPatient: Oh… tired? I suppose sometimes, yes, but I wouldn’t say it’s anything out of the ordinary for someone my age. I haven’t really kept track of it. I’m able to manage, mostly. Do you mean tired during the day, or more worn out in general?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to overstate it. At the senior center meetings, or when I’m sitting through the announcements at church, I get this sort of ants-in-my-chair feeling. I’ll shift around, or get up for coffee, or straighten the papers in front of me just to get through it. I hope that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well… I don’t think I worry more than most people, really. I mean, there are the usual little things—did I remember this or that, is everything in order—but I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m terribly anxious all the time.\n\nI suppose I haven’t paid close attention to it. Is that the sort of worry you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… hmm. I don’t think so, not in any big way. At least, I don’t think I am.\n\nIs that what you mean, like dwelling on it?\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts that you feel compelled to act on?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts I feel I have to act on… I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I don’t think I have anything like that, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, I might remember something I need to do and want to get up and handle it, but I don’t think that’s what you mean, is it?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts about the future?\n\nPatient: The future… no, I don’t think I’m especially preoccupied with it. I mean, I think about practical things now and then, like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say it takes over my mind or anything like that.\n\nAt least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well… I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I do try to keep myself in check.

But yes, I suppose it has caused a little trouble. At the senior center, after meetings, I’ve had to apologize a few times because I guess I came across as too restless or too pushy. And one friend suggested I maybe take a smaller role at the sign-up table, because she said my manner was making things a bit tense.

I felt badly about that. I don’t mean to make anyone uncomfortable, you know?

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

I wouldn’t say I’m terribly impulsive, exactly… but yes, sometimes it comes out in conversation.

Like in the exhibit planning meetings, I’ll answer or toss in my idea before the other person has quite finished explaining. Then I realize I’ve stepped on their words, and I feel embarrassed. I don’t mean to be rude. It just sort of pops out before I’ve thought it through.

Puttering around the house, fixing little things, arranging this or that, saying yes to one more volunteer thing. \n\nI know that may not sound terrible, and I do like being useful, but even at my age I seem to have a hard time just sitting down and being still. I wasn’t sure if that’s worth coming in for, but it’s been on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious or worried when you're not doing something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I don’t sit there worrying, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more just… I feel better if my hands are busy, I suppose. But I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be trying to avoid something by keeping yourself busy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least, I’m not aware of avoiding anything in particular. It’s more that I just seem to be always puttering or fixing little things, arranging something, or saying yes to one more volunteer thing. \n\nEven now, at 87, I find it hard to just sit down and be still. I haven’t really kept track of it. I’m able to manage, mostly. Do you mean tired during the day, or more worn out in general?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to overstate it. At the senior center meetings, or when I’m sitting through the announcements at church, I get this sort of ants-in-my-chair feeling. I’ll shift around, or get up for coffee, or straighten the papers in front of me just to get through it. I hope that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well… I don’t think I worry more than most people, really. I mean, there are the usual little things—did I remember this or that, is everything in order—but I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m terribly anxious all the time.\n\nI suppose I haven’t paid close attention to it. Is that the sort of worry you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… hmm. I don’t think so, not in any big way. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty sitting still and constant need to be doing something significantly interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I do try to keep myself in check.\n\nBut yes, I suppose it has caused a little trouble. At the senior center, after meetings, I’ve had to apologize a few times because I guess I came across as too restless or too pushy. I don’t mean to make anyone uncomfortable, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done perfectly, or that if you don't do them, they won't be done correctly?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Is that the sort of worry you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… hmm. I don’t think so, not in any big way. I mean, I suppose everyone has little regrets—things you wish you’d handled a bit better—but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around feeling guilty all the time. At least, I don’t think I am.\n\nIs that what you mean, like dwelling on it?\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts that you feel compelled to act on?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts I feel I have to act on… I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I don’t think I have anything like that, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, I might remember something I need to do and want to get up and handle it, but I don’t think that’s what you mean, is it?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts about the future?\n\nPatient: The future… no, I don’t think I’m especially preoccupied with it. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty sitting still and constant need to be doing something significantly interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I do try to keep myself in check.\n\nBut yes, I suppose it has caused a little trouble. At the senior center, after meetings, I’ve had to apologize a few times because I guess I came across as too restless or too pushy. And one friend suggested I maybe take a smaller role at the sign-up table, because she said my manner was making things a bit tense.\n\nI felt badly about that. I don’t mean to make anyone uncomfortable, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done perfectly, or that if you don't do them, they won't be done correctly?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I like things done nicely, of course, but I wouldn’t say I need them perfect or that only I can do them right.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that in myself. If something isn’t done exactly my way, I can usually let it be. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m terribly impulsive, exactly… but yes, sometimes it comes out in conversation.\n\nLike in the exhibit planning meetings, I’ll answer or toss in my idea before the other person has quite finished explaining. Then I realize I’ve stepped on their words, and I feel embarrassed. I don’t mean to be rude. It just sort of pops out before I’ve thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be in charge of things to make sure they get done right?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I feel I have to be in charge for that reason. Yes… I suppose I do, a bit. I don’t like to admit that, because it sounds childish, but waiting can make me feel impatient.

I mean, there are the usual little things—did I remember this or that, is everything in order—but I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m terribly anxious all the time.\n\nI suppose I haven’t paid close attention to it. Is that the sort of worry you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… hmm. I don’t think so, not in any big way. I mean, I suppose everyone has little regrets—things you wish you’d handled a bit better—but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around feeling guilty all the time. At least, I don’t think I am.\n\nIs that what you mean, like dwelling on it?\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts that you feel compelled to act on?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts I feel I have to act on… I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I don’t think I have anything like that, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, I might remember something I need to do and want to get up and handle it, but I don’t think that’s what you mean, is it?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts about the future?\n\nPatient: The future… no, I don’t think I’m especially preoccupied with it. I mean, I think about practical things now and then, like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say it takes over my mind or anything like that.\n\nAt least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty sitting still and constant need to be doing something significantly interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I do try to keep myself in check.\n\nBut yes, I suppose it has caused a little trouble. At the senior center, after meetings, I’ve had to apologize a few times because I guess I came across as too restless or too pushy. And one friend suggested I maybe take a smaller role at the sign-up table, because she said my manner was making things a bit tense.\n\nI felt badly about that. I don’t mean to make anyone uncomfortable, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done perfectly, or that if you don't do them, they won't be done correctly?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I like things done nicely, of course, but I wouldn’t say I need them perfect or that only I can do them right.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that in myself. If something isn’t done exactly my way, I can usually let it be. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m terribly impulsive, exactly… but yes, sometimes it comes out in conversation.\n\nLike in the exhibit planning meetings, I’ll answer or toss in my idea before the other person has quite finished explaining. Then I realize I’ve stepped on their words, and I feel embarrassed. I don’t mean to be rude. It just sort of pops out before I’ve thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be in charge of things to make sure they get done right?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I feel I have to be in charge for that reason. I mean, I may offer help, and sometimes I probably get a bit too involved, but it’s not because I think others can’t do it right.\n\nAt least, I haven’t thought of it that way. If someone else is handling something, I can usually let them. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly tapping or anything like that, but when I’m sitting still for a while—like at the senior center meetings, or during the announcements at church—I get this sort of ants-in-my-chair feeling.\n\nI’ll shift around, or get up for coffee if that’s acceptable, or straighten the papers in front of me just to have something to do. It’s not terrible, I don’t think, but it does happen. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting for your turn in lines or when speaking with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, a bit. I don’t like to admit that, because it sounds childish, but waiting can make me feel impatient.\n\nLike if there’s a sign-up line, or we’re waiting for supplies to be handed out, I’ll catch myself getting fidgety and a little short inside. I might sigh, or check my watch, or sort of edge forward when really I should just stand there and wait my turn like everyone else.\n\nI try not to make it obvious, but yes, it does happen.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

At the senior center, after meetings, I’ve had to apologize a few times because I guess I came across as too restless or too pushy. And one friend suggested I maybe take a smaller role at the sign-up table, because she said my manner was making things a bit tense.\n\nI felt badly about that. I don’t mean to make anyone uncomfortable, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done perfectly, or that if you don't do them, they won't be done correctly?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I like things done nicely, of course, but I wouldn’t say I need them perfect or that only I can do them right.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that in myself. If something isn’t done exactly my way, I can usually let it be. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m terribly impulsive, exactly… but yes, sometimes it comes out in conversation.\n\nLike in the exhibit planning meetings, I’ll answer or toss in my idea before the other person has quite finished explaining. Then I realize I’ve stepped on their words, and I feel embarrassed. I don’t mean to be rude. It just sort of pops out before I’ve thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be in charge of things to make sure they get done right?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I feel I have to be in charge for that reason. I mean, I may offer help, and sometimes I probably get a bit too involved, but it’s not because I think others can’t do it right.\n\nAt least, I haven’t thought of it that way. If someone else is handling something, I can usually let them. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly tapping or anything like that, but when I’m sitting still for a while—like at the senior center meetings, or during the announcements at church—I get this sort of ants-in-my-chair feeling.\n\nI’ll shift around, or get up for coffee if that’s acceptable, or straighten the papers in front of me just to have something to do. It’s not terrible, I don’t think, but it does happen. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting for your turn in lines or when speaking with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, a bit. I don’t like to admit that, because it sounds childish, but waiting can make me feel impatient.\n\nLike if there’s a sign-up line, or we’re waiting for supplies to be handed out, I’ll catch myself getting fidgety and a little short inside. I might sigh, or check my watch, or sort of edge forward when really I should just stand there and wait my turn like everyone else.\n\nI try not to make it obvious, but yes, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with organization or time management in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them significant problems, no. I mean, at my age I do keep lists and a calendar, and I like things written down so I don’t forget, but I think that’s fairly normal.\n\nSometimes I may take a little longer to get myself ready or sort papers, but I’ve managed my daily routines well enough. So… no, I don’t think organization or time management has been a major issue for me. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I’ve noticed over the past year or so that I’m just always doing something. Puttering around the house, fixing little things, arranging this or that, saying yes to one more volunteer thing. \n\nI know that may not sound terrible, and I do like being useful, but even at my age I seem to have a hard time just sitting down and being still. I wasn’t sure if that’s worth coming in for, but it’s been on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious or worried when you're not doing something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I don’t sit there worrying, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more just… I feel better if my hands are busy, I suppose. But I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. I don’t mean to make anyone uncomfortable, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done perfectly, or that if you don't do them, they won't be done correctly?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I like things done nicely, of course, but I wouldn’t say I need them perfect or that only I can do them right.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that in myself. If something isn’t done exactly my way, I can usually let it be. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m terribly impulsive, exactly… but yes, sometimes it comes out in conversation.\n\nLike in the exhibit planning meetings, I’ll answer or toss in my idea before the other person has quite finished explaining. Then I realize I’ve stepped on their words, and I feel embarrassed. I don’t mean to be rude. It just sort of pops out before I’ve thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be in charge of things to make sure they get done right?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I feel I have to be in charge for that reason. I mean, I may offer help, and sometimes I probably get a bit too involved, but it’s not because I think others can’t do it right.\n\nAt least, I haven’t thought of it that way. If someone else is handling something, I can usually let them. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly tapping or anything like that, but when I’m sitting still for a while—like at the senior center meetings, or during the announcements at church—I get this sort of ants-in-my-chair feeling.\n\nI’ll shift around, or get up for coffee if that’s acceptable, or straighten the papers in front of me just to have something to do. It’s not terrible, I don’t think, but it does happen. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting for your turn in lines or when speaking with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, a bit. I don’t like to admit that, because it sounds childish, but waiting can make me feel impatient.\n\nLike if there’s a sign-up line, or we’re waiting for supplies to be handed out, I’ll catch myself getting fidgety and a little short inside. I might sigh, or check my watch, or sort of edge forward when really I should just stand there and wait my turn like everyone else.\n\nI try not to make it obvious, but yes, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with organization or time management in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them significant problems, no. I mean, at my age I do keep lists and a calendar, and I like things written down so I don’t forget, but I think that’s fairly normal.\n\nSometimes I may take a little longer to get myself ready or sort papers, but I’ve managed my daily routines well enough. So… no, I don’t think organization or time management has been a major issue for me. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be doing something is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well… “a lot” of distress might be putting it strongly, but it is part of why I came in, yes.\n\nFor the past year or so I’ve noticed I’m always puttering around—fixing this, arranging that, signing up to help with one more thing. Even at 87, I seem to have trouble just sitting down and being still. I tell myself it’s good to stay active, of course, but sometimes it feels like I can’t quite turn it off, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I’ve noticed over the past year or so that I’m just always doing something. Puttering around the house, fixing little things, arranging this or that, saying yes to one more volunteer thing. \n\nI know that may not sound terrible, and I do like being useful, but even at my age I seem to have a hard time just sitting down and being still. I wasn’t sure if that’s worth coming in for, but it’s been on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious or worried when you're not doing something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I don’t sit there worrying, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more just… I feel better if my hands are busy, I suppose. But I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be trying to avoid something by keeping yourself busy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least, I’m not aware of avoiding anything in particular. It’s more that I just seem to be always puttering or fixing little things, arranging something, or saying yes to one more volunteer thing. \n\nEven now, at 87, I find it hard to just sit down and be still. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired?\n\nPatient: Oh… tired? I suppose sometimes, yes, but I wouldn’t say it’s anything out of the ordinary for someone my age. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty sitting still and constant need to be doing something significantly interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I do try to keep myself in check.\n\nBut yes, I suppose it has caused a little trouble. At the senior center, after meetings, I’ve had to apologize a few times because I guess I came across as too restless or too pushy. And one friend suggested I maybe take a smaller role at the sign-up table, because she said my manner was making things a bit tense.\n\nI felt badly about that. I don’t mean to make anyone uncomfortable, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done perfectly, or that if you don't do them, they won't be done correctly?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I like things done nicely, of course, but I wouldn’t say I need them perfect or that only I can do them right.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that in myself. If something isn’t done exactly my way, I can usually let it be. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m terribly impulsive, exactly… but yes, sometimes it comes out in conversation.\n\nLike in the exhibit planning meetings, I’ll answer or toss in my idea before the other person has quite finished explaining. Then I realize I’ve stepped on their words, and I feel embarrassed. I don’t mean to be rude. It just sort of pops out before I’ve thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be in charge of things to make sure they get done right?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I feel I have to be in charge for that reason. I mean, I may offer help, and sometimes I probably get a bit too involved, but it’s not because I think others can’t do it right.\n\nAt least, I haven’t thought of it that way. If someone else is handling something, I can usually let them. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly tapping or anything like that, but when I’m sitting still for a while—like at the senior center meetings, or during the announcements at church—I get this sort of ants-in-my-chair feeling.\n\nI’ll shift around, or get up for coffee if that’s acceptable, or straighten the papers in front of me just to have something to do. It’s not terrible, I don’t think, but it does happen. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting for your turn in lines or when speaking with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, a bit. I don’t like to admit that, because it sounds childish, but waiting can make me feel impatient.\n\nLike if there’s a sign-up line, or we’re waiting for supplies to be handed out, I’ll catch myself getting fidgety and a little short inside. I might sigh, or check my watch, or sort of edge forward when really I should just stand there and wait my turn like everyone else.\n\nI try not to make it obvious, but yes, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with organization or time management in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them significant problems, no. I mean, at my age I do keep lists and a calendar, and I like things written down so I don’t forget, but I think that’s fairly normal.\n\nSometimes I may take a little longer to get myself ready or sort papers, but I’ve managed my daily routines well enough. So… no, I don’t think organization or time management has been a major issue for me. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be doing something is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well… “a lot” of distress might be putting it strongly, but it is part of why I came in, yes.\n\nFor the past year or so I’ve noticed I’m always puttering around—fixing this, arranging that, signing up to help with one more thing. Even at 87, I seem to have trouble just sitting down and being still. I tell myself it’s good to stay active, of course, but sometimes it feels like I can’t quite turn it off, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant symptoms of inattention, such as difficulty sustaining focus during tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in any serious way.\n\nI can get distracted now and then, like anyone, especially if something is dull, but I haven’t noticed a real problem with keeping my attention on tasks. If I’m reading, doing paperwork, or helping with something, I can usually stay with it well enough.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to overstate that. Does that answer it properly?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Is that the sort of worry you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… hmm. I don’t think so, not in any big way. I mean, I suppose everyone has little regrets—things you wish you’d handled a bit better—but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around feeling guilty all the time. At least, I don’t think I am.\n\nIs that what you mean, like dwelling on it?\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts that you feel compelled to act on?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts I feel I have to act on… I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I don’t think I have anything like that, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, I might remember something I need to do and want to get up and handle it, but I don’t think that’s what you mean, is it?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts about the future?\n\nPatient: The future… no, I don’t think I’m especially preoccupied with it. I mean, I think about practical things now and then, like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say it takes over my mind or anything like that.\n\nAt least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty sitting still and constant need to be doing something significantly interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I do try to keep myself in check.\n\nBut yes, I suppose it has caused a little trouble. At the senior center, after meetings, I’ve had to apologize a few times because I guess I came across as too restless or too pushy. And one friend suggested I maybe take a smaller role at the sign-up table, because she said my manner was making things a bit tense.\n\nI felt badly about that. I don’t mean to make anyone uncomfortable, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done perfectly, or that if you don't do them, they won't be done correctly?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I like things done nicely, of course, but I wouldn’t say I need them perfect or that only I can do them right.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that in myself. If something isn’t done exactly my way, I can usually let it be. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m terribly impulsive, exactly… but yes, sometimes it comes out in conversation.\n\nLike in the exhibit planning meetings, I’ll answer or toss in my idea before the other person has quite finished explaining. Then I realize I’ve stepped on their words, and I feel embarrassed. I don’t mean to be rude. It just sort of pops out before I’ve thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be in charge of things to make sure they get done right?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I feel I have to be in charge for that reason. I mean, I may offer help, and sometimes I probably get a bit too involved, but it’s not because I think others can’t do it right.\n\nAt least, I haven’t thought of it that way. If someone else is handling something, I can usually let them. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly tapping or anything like that, but when I’m sitting still for a while—like at the senior center meetings, or during the announcements at church—I get this sort of ants-in-my-chair feeling.\n\nI’ll shift around, or get up for coffee if that’s acceptable, or straighten the papers in front of me just to have something to do. It’s not terrible, I don’t think, but it does happen. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting for your turn in lines or when speaking with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, a bit. I don’t like to admit that, because it sounds childish, but waiting can make me feel impatient.\n\nLike if there’s a sign-up line, or we’re waiting for supplies to be handed out, I’ll catch myself getting fidgety and a little short inside. I might sigh, or check my watch, or sort of edge forward when really I should just stand there and wait my turn like everyone else.\n\nI try not to make it obvious, but yes, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with organization or time management in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them significant problems, no. I mean, at my age I do keep lists and a calendar, and I like things written down so I don’t forget, but I think that’s fairly normal.\n\nSometimes I may take a little longer to get myself ready or sort papers, but I’ve managed my daily routines well enough. So… no, I don’t think organization or time management has been a major issue for me. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be doing something is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well… “a lot” of distress might be putting it strongly, but it is part of why I came in, yes.\n\nFor the past year or so I’ve noticed I’m always puttering around—fixing this, arranging that, signing up to help with one more thing. Even at 87, I seem to have trouble just sitting down and being still. I tell myself it’s good to stay active, of course, but sometimes it feels like I can’t quite turn it off, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant symptoms of inattention, such as difficulty sustaining focus during tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in any serious way.\n\nI can get distracted now and then, like anyone, especially if something is dull, but I haven’t noticed a real problem with keeping my attention on tasks. If I’m reading, doing paperwork, or helping with something, I can usually stay with it well enough.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to overstate that. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or treated for ADHD?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t believe I’ve ever been diagnosed with that or treated for it. Not that I can recall, anyway.\n\nI’ve talked with doctors over the years about ordinary health things, of course, but nothing specifically for ADHD. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
